- [ ] **Power budget (CLAUDE.md §4.5) — TMS has no electrical spec, T2-peak figure never included it (NP-PWR-BUDGET-001 Rev 1, 2026-08-02)** — Design study reversed the direction every other power/thermal document uses: instead of taking CLAUDE.md §4.5 as a given input, derived what the budget should be from actual safety ceilings. **T1 (PBM):** the existing "~6 concurrent tiles" rule (NP-HW-HEXTILE-001 §9) is power-budget-derived, not thermally derived; an independent first-order thermal estimate off the existing single-tile CFD model (NP-THERM-CFD-R1-001) brackets 4–8 tiles — close enough that T1's figures can likely stand pending real multi-tile CFD (OI-PWR-01), but flags that clustered-montage activation (e.g. bilateral DLPFC) concentrates residual cavity heat more than the flat rule assumes. **T2 (the bigger finding):** TMS coil drive has **zero** power/energy specification anywhere in the document tree, and is absent from the hub power architecture and the thermal power-budget table — T2-peak (70–74W) is explicitly sized for the 1170nm laser zone only. An order-of-magnitude physics estimate (E∝B² scaling from general clinical rTMS device figures) puts average power during a 10Hz pulse train at ~15–400W depending where in NeurOne's own stated 0.1–0.5T field range the coil runs — at the top of that range, TMS alone exceeds the entire current T2-peak envelope. Also flags that 0.1–0.5T may itself be below what commercial figure-8 coils need for the 120% RMT protocols the evidence base cites (separate EM coil-design question, OI-PWR-02). **RESOLVED IN PART 2026-09-15 — `SPEC-TMS-01…05` (`NP-PWR-THERM-001` §8) gives the coil an electrical specification**, derived from a coil geometry rather than from E ∝ B² scaling (`OI-PWR-03` answered): figure-8, two 25 mm wings in series, 10 turns each, **L = 18.2 µH**, a **28 µF / 1,600 V** bank, **36.0 J/pulse at 0.5 T**, a **142 µs** ring, **1,989 A** peak, and **169 W** of average recharge at 10 Hz biphasic-with-recovery (**423 W** monophasic, which no PD class delivers — hence the topology is specified rather than left open). **It reproduces §4.1's scaling estimate at both ends of the field range from inputs the estimate shares none of**, and `check-power-envelope.ts --validate` pins that. **`OI-PWR-02` is confirmed, quantified and now BLOCKING:** at 0.5 T the coil reaches **17 % of a clinical figure-8's maximum cortical field and is 4.1× short of 120 % RMT**, the shortfall being **depth falloff of a 25 mm wing** (which §4.2 does not charge it for) and not the peak-field comparison §4.2 does make — the 1.8× this coil *gains* on pulse width is already credited. Clinical equivalence at this geometry needs **2.06 T → 613 J/pulse → 2,886 W**, so **the TMS supply question has two answers 17× apart** and §9's `T2 + TMS ~176 W / 240 W EPR` row is written at the lower one **and labelled**. **The binding residual is not power at all: `OI-PWRTH-01` (BLOCKING) — 33 W of I²R inside a coil at the scalp under the same 42 °C applied-part limit, an adiabatic budget of 516 pulses against a 3,000-pulse protocol, no cooling path specified anywhere, and a non-conductive window beneath that forecloses a metallic spreader.** **Routed to principal decision (OI-PWR-04):** does TMS share the main battery-buffered rail (needs a purpose-built capacitor bank + likely >100W EPR PD contract) or run external/mains-tethered for T2 sessions (excludes TMS from Mode 3 autonomous operation)? Does not modify CLAUDE.md §4.5 pending that decision. See `docs/np_pwr_budget_001.md`.
- [x] **OI-CHARGE-04: tDCS electrode-area assumption diverged between the app pre-flight check and the safety MCU enforcer (raised 2026-08-05, RESOLVED 2026-09-09 — decision owner: Regulatory / Safety SW)** — The 40 µC/cm² charge-density limit was computed against **two different electrode areas**. iOS `NPHardwareLimits.tdcsDefaultElectrodeAreaCm2 = 35.0` (per DHF Rev 11, "a standard tDCS sponge pad") drove the app's pre-flight validation; the safety MCU's `NP_ELECTRODE_AREA_CM2 = 25` (per OI-CHARGE-02) drove the hard cutoff. Not a safety hole — the MCU owns the enable GPIO — but a *fidelity* defect: the app accepted protocols the MCU cut short mid-session. **Three corrections to the original entry, found while resolving it:** (a) **the divergence was ~2.8×, not the 1.4× recorded.** The app did not merely use a different area, it used a different *model*: `NPProtocolValidator` divided by `35 × electrodeCount`, summing every electrode's area into one denominator, where charge density is a **per-electrode** quantity — the full session current passes through each electrode of a pair. For one pair that is 70 cm² against the MCU's 25, so a session ran to ~36% of its authored duration, not 71%; at three pairs (210 cm²) it was ~12%. **Harmonizing the constant alone would have left most of the gap in place**, which is why option (1) was not taken. (b) **Three app sites carried it, not two.** `app/android/.../NPHardwareLimits.kt` had the same `TDCS_DEFAULT_ELECTRODE_AREA_CM2 = 35.0` with **no divergence note at all** — the original entry's "documented in-place at both app sites" counted iOS and the web comment. (c) **Option (2) was far cheaper than costed.** The entry judged a per-protocol authored area "real firmware work" touching the wire format and the Class C charge monitor; most of that had already landed under OI-CHARGE-02/-03. `np_safety_chan_limit_cmd_t` already carried `area_mcm2[14]` — every channel, not just HD-tDCS — and `np_charge_monitor_set_channel_area_mcm2()` was already generic over channel index. **Closed by option (2), authored geometry.** `np_mod_tdcs_params_t` gains `electrode_area_mcm2` (6 → 8 bytes, `NP_HUB_PROTO_VERSION` 2 → 3); the session runner populates `area_mcm2[NP_SAFETY_CH_TDCS]` from the signed descriptor exactly as it already did for HD-tDCS; the charge monitor's fail-safe geometry gate now covers TDCS on its own heartbeat bit (`NP_SESSION_STATUS_GEOM_REQ_TDCS`, bit 3). **A second bit rather than a widening of bit 2, deliberately:** the gates are per-channel, and one shared flag would have let a tDCS geometry declaration gate off clinical tACS, which shares the CLIN_STIM enable bit and declares no geometry. **Neither 25 nor 35 was changed.** 25 remains the MCU's fallback for channels that declare no geometry — which tDCS no longer is, because the gate holds the channel off rather than falling back — and 35 became the app's *authoring default* for a standard sponge pad, transmitted and enforced instead of assumed at one end. The DHF Rev 11 record therefore stands rather than needing the change note option (1) would have required. **Web gained the check** (`protocolValidator.ts`): it had carried `tdcsMaxChargeDensityUCcm2: 40.0` and checked nothing, and could author and compile a protocol the other two clients rejected. **Verified:** 34/34 firmware host tests, 309/309 web tests (6 new charge-density tests pinning the per-electrode model, 2 new `.npps` round-trip tests), all 9 CI gates. iOS and Android are unbuilt here — no Swift toolchain and no Android SDK in this environment — so `ios-ci.yml` and `:core:testDebugUnitTest` are what compile them; every new symbol was checked against its declaration by hand. **Raised while closing this: `OI-CHARGE-05` below** — the app and the safety MCU enforce charge-density ceilings **1000× apart** (the app's `I(mA)×t(s)/A(cm²)` yields mC/cm² and is compared against 40; the MCU enforces 40 µC/cm²), the ceiling suits a pulsed waveform tDCS does not have, and the interlock is inert because the hub never sends `current_ua[]`. **That 1000× gap is the larger divergence between app and enforcer — larger than the 2.8× this item fixed.** OI-CHARGE-04 neither depends on nor fixes it: the declared-geometry path carries whatever ceiling OI-CHARGE-05 settles on, and the density constant never crosses SPI.

- [x] **`OI-CHARGE-05`: the app and the safety MCU enforce charge-density ceilings 1000× apart, the ceiling suits a waveform tDCS does not have, and the interlock is not live (raised 2026-09-09, revised same day, RESOLVED 2026-09-15 — decision owner: Regulatory / Safety SW)** — **CLOSED on all four parts; full record in `completed-decisions.md`.** (a) The two ceilings were never the same quantity: `tdcsMaxChargeDensityUCcm2` is renamed `tdcsMaxSessionChargeDensityMCcm2` on all three clients, and both figures survive attached to their waveform class. (b) `np_charge_monitor.c` is waveform-aware — DC channels accumulate against a per-session mC/cm² budget, charge-balanced channels get a per-heartbeat per-phase µC/cm² predicate and never accumulate; a new 76-byte `np_safety_chan_wave_cmd_t` carries the classification (the ceilings themselves stay on the Class C MCU), and an undeclared electrical channel is held out of `granted_mask`. (c) OI-CHARGE-01's hub half is wired: modality modules publish commanded current beside the enable they already request, so the interlock enforces for the first time. (d) The circular citation chain is broken — `NP-DT-001` §3.2.1 now derives **DI-SAFE-01** (150 mC/cm² per session, DC) and **DI-SAFE-01a** (40 µC/cm² per phase, pulsed) from sources outside this repository's own assertions, and **2 mA × 20 min is available**, so the shipped predefined library is admissible. The 150 is **PROVISIONAL pending Regulatory sign-off — `OI-CHARGE-06`**; the provisional product-electrode areas are `OI-CHARGE-07`. Original diagnosis retained below for the record. — **This entry replaces a first draft written the same day that got the diagnosis substantially wrong. What that draft said, and why it was wrong, is recorded at the end so the error is not silently absorbed.** Three defects, separable, none of them architectural and none touching hardware.

  **(a) The two sides enforce ceilings 1000× apart, and the app's is the clinically recognised one.** All three clients compute `I(mA) × t(s) / A(cm²)`. `mA × s = mC`, so **that expression yields mC/cm²** — and it is compared against `40`. The app has therefore been enforcing **40 mC/cm²**, which is the standard human tDCS figure (Nitsche/Bikson; Liebetanz 2009 measured rat lesions well above it). Only the *message* said "µC/cm²". The safety MCU genuinely enforces 40 µC/cm²: `NP_CHARGE_LIMIT_UC_CM2 = 40` against an accumulator that really is in nC (`current_ua[µA] × dt_us[µs] / 1000` → nC), so a 35 cm² pad gets a **1.4 mC** budget — 2 mA exhausts it in 0.7 s, less than the 30 s ramp the firmware enforces as a minimum. **This 1000× gap, not OI-CHARGE-04's 2.8× area/model gap, is the dominant divergence between app and enforcer.** Which side is right is the decision: the µC figure is a real *per-phase* limit (Shannon/McCreery), the mC figure is a real *per-session DC* limit, and the numeral being 40 in both is what made the confusion invisible.

  **(b) The same constant is applied to charge-balanced waveforms, where the accumulated quantity is meaningless.** `np_safety_main.c` accumulates `|I| × dt` for **every** granted channel with a non-zero commanded current. BES/tACS, VNS, cervical VNS and clinical tACS are all charge-balanced biphasic (CLAUDE.md §3), so net delivered charge is ~zero and a session-cumulative integral of magnitude is not a physical dose. For those channels the meaningful quantity is charge **per phase** — amplitude × pulse width — which is exactly what 40 µC/cm² is the right ceiling for. As written they would trip in **0.4–1.0 s** at their rated currents against the 25 cm² default budget (1 mA → 200 µC/beat, 2 mA → 400, 4 mA → 800, against 1000 µC). **So this is not a tDCS-only item: it reaches every electrical modality in both tiers.**

  **(c) The interlock is not enforcing anything today — and the hazard analysis already named this failure, under the wrong cause (`OI-FMEA-07`).** `np_hub_control_main.c:164` calls `np_safety_spi_heartbeat(state, req_mask, NULL, 0U)` — no `current_ua[]`, `channel_count = 0` — so the MCU's accumulate loop never runs. The call site says so itself: *"per-channel commanded-current wiring into the heartbeat is a separate charge-monitor integration (OI-CHARGE-01 hub side)"*. **`OI-CHARGE-01` is logged CLOSED but only its MCU half was wired.** This is why (a) and (b) have never been noticed — nothing has ever reached the limit. It also means the 40 µC/cm² control that `NP-DT-001` DI-SAFE-01, `NP-FMEA-001` SW01-M03 and `NP-FW-BENCH-001` (*"never bypassable"*) all describe as enforced **is currently inert**, which is a documentation-accuracy problem independent of the numbers. **`NP-FMEA-001` FMEA-M03-03 scores exactly this effect** — *"Charge density limit never reached; patient receives unlimited charge"* — at **S5/P2 = 10, UNACCEPTABLE initial**, but attributes it to a stuck-low ADC that does not exist and mitigates it with an ADC self-test that was never built. That gap between the hazard analysis and the design is `OI-FMEA-07`, raised separately because it is true regardless of what this item decides about the ceiling.

  **(d) The ceiling has no source. The citation chain is circular.** Traced 2026-09-09 because the resolution shape depends on whether there is a prior decision to overturn. There is not. `NP-DT-001` **DI-SAFE-01 — a design *input*** — lists its Source as *"CLAUDE.md §4.2; NP-SW-001 SW01-M03"*; `NP-SW-001` SW01-M03 states *"aborts at 95% of 40µC/cm²"* with no source; CLAUDE.md §3 and §4.2 state it as a locked invariant with no source. **No paper, no standard, no predicate device anywhere in the document tree.** `IEC 60601-2-10` is cited at DI-REG-02 but for *"current limits and waveform parameters"*, its verification **VE-11 is Open** (accredited-lab testing planned Month 10–14), and it is a nerve/muscle stimulator *output* standard that does not set a cortical charge-density ceiling. Git cannot help: the repository's history begins at `9841aa4` with the figure already present. **Consequence for how this item is worked: OI-CHARGE-05 is a re-derivation, not a reversal.** Regulatory / Safety SW is not being asked to overturn a recorded judgement — no basis was ever written down — but to establish one for the first time, per waveform class. That is cheaper to decide and more expensive to evidence than a units fix, and under 21 CFR 820.30(c) a design input whose source is the document asserting it is not yet a design input.

  **Where the number most likely came from — three independent tells, all pointing at the pulsed literature**, where **40 µC/cm² per phase is a real and correct figure** (Shannon/McCreery-type limits for pulsed neural stimulation): (i) `docs/np_fw_hd_001.md` §, the **only** place in the tree that names the quantity precisely, calls it **"Per-phase charge density"**; (ii) `docs/ABBREVIATIONS.md` defines **tDCS** as *"0.1–2mA DC, **charge-balanced biphasic pulses**"* — self-contradictory, since tDCS is DC by definition and "charge-balanced biphasic" is the BES/tACS/VNS description, so the pulsed waveform had already been pasted onto tDCS in the glossary; (iii) `NP-FMEA-001` §SW01-M03 describes **one** integrator *"for all electrical stimulation modalities (tDCS, BES/tACS, VNS, cervical VNS)"* — the conflation is in the hazard analysis itself, not only in the code. The reading that fits every piece of evidence: a correct per-phase pulsed limit was adopted once, applied to the whole electrical tier, and survived review because **40 is plausible in both readings** — 40 µC/cm² per phase (pulsed) and 40 mC/cm² per session (DC tDCS) are both real numbers in this literature.

  **Resolution shape (not decided here).** No hardware consequence: current source, electrodes, 30 s ramp, ≤2 mA, ≤3 pairs, the 42/62 °C thermal cutoffs and the impedance interlock are all independent and unaffected — no BOM, tooling or schematic change. (1) Make the charge model **waveform-aware**: DC channels get a per-session charge-density ceiling in mC/cm²; pulsed/AC channels get a per-phase ceiling in µC/cm² derived from amplitude × pulse width rather than an integral over session time. The hub already knows which modality owns each channel, and since OI-CHARGE-04 every channel has a declared electrode area to divide by. (2) Correct the app's unit label. (3) Complete OI-CHARGE-01's hub half so the interlock actually enforces. (4) Then set the ceiling.

  **The one genuinely clinical question, with a hard consequence to weigh now:** at 40 mC/cm² on 35 cm² pads, **2 mA × 20 min is not available** (68.6 mC/cm²). Keeping it needs either ~**60 cm²** pads (2 × 1200 / 60 = 40 exactly) or a ceiling above 68.6 with a citation behind it. This is a real limit, not an artifact — Batsikadze 2013 at 2 mA is above the classic Nitsche ceiling, which the field has actually debated. **13 of the 14 shipped predefined tDCS protocols exceed 40 mC/cm²** (51.4 to 102.9; the sole pass, `clinical-33-tdcs-aphasia`, is the only 1 mA protocol in the set), so this decision sets the shape of the Grade-A library, and the library currently assumes 2 mA is available. **Do not resolve it by inflating declared pad areas** — those are physical facts about the hardware, and OI-CHARGE-04 exists to stop software assuming them.

  **What the superseded first draft claimed, and the corrections:** it said the 40 µC/cm² ceiling was ~1000× below the whole evidence base **and treated both sides as sharing that error** — wrong, the app's threshold is effectively 40 mC/cm² and only its label was off, so the finding is a 1000× *divergence*, not a shared 1000× understatement. It cited Nitsche & Paulus 2000 as "8,571 µC/cm², 214× the ceiling" — the number is right but it is **8.57 mC/cm², which passes a 40 mC/cm² ceiling comfortably**; only the 2 mA protocols fail. And it stated the shipped protocols "were already being cut off at ~0.7 s of a 20-minute session" — they were not, because of (c): nothing is cut off today. The draft also missed (b) and (c) entirely.
- [x] **`OI-FMEA-07`: NP-FMEA-001 §SW01-M03 does not analyse the charge monitor that exists — two of its three failure modes are about an ADC the design does not have, and its UNACCEPTABLE-initial hazard is the system's live state (raised 2026-09-09, RESOLVED 2026-09-15 — decision owner: Quality / Safety SW)** — **§3.3 re-authored in NP-FMEA-001 Rev 7 and risk scores CHANGED**, which Revs 5 and 6 deliberately did not do. The two ADC failure modes are replaced by the hazard they were standing in for — **delivered current diverging from commanded** (new FMEA-M03-02) — and FMEA-M03-03 keeps its S5/P2 = 10 UNACCEPTABLE-initial score with the cause corrected to *commanded current never reaching the monitor*, now mitigated by controls that exist: the wired hub publish, the fail-closed waveform-declaration gate, and the SW01-M02 watchdog. FMEA-M03-06 added for the wrong-ceiling hazard. The module is named a **commanded-dose** control throughout and the substitution's real justification — **signature independence, not privacy** — is stated for the first time. `OI-FMEA-04` closed NOT APPLICABLE (no ADC to bench-validate). **Two things deliberately NOT closed here and carried forward:** the hub's commanded-versus-delivered cross-check and SHDR divergence flag are specified but **not built**, so FMEA-M03-02's residual rating is recorded in §4 as explicitly conditional on them (`OI-FMEA-09`); and the 95% ramp-down margin both requirement documents specified and neither implementation has is `OI-FMEA-08`. Original analysis retained below. — Found while tracing `OI-CHARGE-05`'s provenance. **Independent of the ceiling value**: it would remain true if 40 µC/cm² were correct. `NP-FMEA-001` §3.3 and `NP-SW-001` SW01-M03 describe a module that was never built, and the hazard analysis of record therefore scores mitigations that do not exist.

  | NP-FMEA-001 §3.3 / NP-SW-001 says | `firmware/safety_mcu/src/np_charge_monitor.c` does |
  |---|---|
  | Module is `np_charge_density.c/.h` | **No such file.** The module is `np_charge_monitor.c` |
  | *"a dedicated current-sense ADC channel (1 kHz sample rate)"* | **No ADC.** Accumulates *commanded* current from the 200 ms SPI heartbeat — **5 Hz, and not measured** |
  | Aborts at *"95% of the hardware safety limit"*, the margin *"provides margin for the abort ramp-down period"* | Trips at **100%** (`s_charge_nc[ch] >= s_limit_nc[ch]`). No 95% anywhere in the safety MCU |
  | *"Response time is within one PWM period (<25 µs at 40 kHz)"* | Up to **200 ms** — the monitor ticks on the heartbeat loop. Four orders of magnitude |
  | FMEA-M03-01: accumulator is `uint32_t`, mitigated by *"roll-over detection"* plus *"unit test validates 32-bit boundary"* | `uint64_t`, **no roll-over detection**, and **no such unit test** |

  **The two most serious rows are the ones that cannot be fixed by editing the FMEA.** FMEA-M03-02 (*"ADC read returns saturated value"*) and **FMEA-M03-03** (*"ADC read returns 0x000 (stuck low) — no charge accumulates in monitor"*, effect: *"Charge density limit never reached; patient receives unlimited charge"*, **S5/P2 = 10, UNACCEPTABLE initial**) are both hazards *of an ADC*. Their mitigations — ADC self-test at session start, 2-point calibration, saturation detection, stuck-low blocking session enable — are **fictional for this architecture**, because the monitor reads a commanded value over SPI and has nothing to self-test. **So an UNACCEPTABLE-initial hazard is carried as mitigated to acceptable by a control that was never built and cannot be built as described.**

  **And FMEA-M03-03's effect line describes the live system.** Per `OI-CHARGE-05` (c), `np_hub_control_main.c` calls the heartbeat with `current_ua = NULL, channel_count = 0`, so no charge accumulates and the limit is never reached — arrived at through a different mechanism than a stuck ADC, with the identical effect the FMEA scores S5. The hazard analysis named the failure and then analysed the wrong cause.

  **What the commanded-current substitution does and does not cost — CORRECTED 2026-09-09.** `np_charge_monitor.c` records the substitution as deliberate: commanded current is SHDR, whereas *"ADC-measured actual current … would be UHDR-class"*. **A first draft of this item read that as the UHDR/SHDR boundary forbidding a delivered-dose interlock. That was wrong, and the counter-example is in the same header file.** The `np_safety_imp_report_t` privacy gate (`firmware/common/include/np_spi_wire_types.h` §) already moves a UHDR-class measurement across this exact boundary: *"`cvns_kohm_x100[]` is measured tissue impedance → UHDR (user biology). It is transferred device-internally (MCU → hub) purely for cross-validation and is **NEVER written to SHDR**. Only a per-device **DIVERGENCE FLAG** (a device-condition signal, no biology) may be recorded to SHDR."* **So the rule is not "UHDR-class data may not cross SPI" — it is "UHDR-class data may not land in SHDR",** and the accepted pattern for the former is already implemented and already reviewed. Privacy is not what blocks a delivered-dose control.

  **What actually constrains it — two things, and only the second is expensive.** (i) **Signature independence, not privacy.** Commanded current originates in a cryptographically signed session descriptor whose hash the MCU verifies (`np_safety_sig_cmd_t`); a *measured* value cannot be signed in advance. A Class C interlock cutting off against a number the Class B hub measures and transmits would inherit Class B's integrity — a faulty or compromised hub could under-report and the MCU could not tell. **That is a real argument for keeping the hard cutoff on commanded current, and it is the argument the design should have recorded instead of the privacy one.** (ii) **Hardware, for the strong form only.** The safety MCU's ADC1 channels are fully allocated — six NTC thermal domains and six impedance channels, including `NP_IMP1_ADC_CH = 5` for tDCS — and there is **no current-sense channel**. For the MCU to measure delivered current *itself* needs a sense resistor wired to an MCU ADC input: the only new hardware this whole thread implies, landing on the pin budget `OI-HEXTILE-13` already contests.

  **Recommended resolution (principal proposal 2026-09-09; needs no new hardware).** Track both doses, write both to UHDR, and let only a biology-free flag reach SHDR — the CVNS pattern, reused: (1) the **safety MCU keeps the hard cutoff on commanded current**, which stays signed, verifiable and independent of the hub; (2) the **hub logs both, at their own natural rates** — delivered is a *time series* (the existing periodic `NP_LOG_TAG_UHDR_STIM` telemetry record, which already carries delivered current, charge and impedance to UHDR: *"UHDR: delivered current and charge — treatment parameters received"*), while commanded is an *event*: it changes only when a command is issued — session start, interval transition, ramp step — so it is recorded **once per command, not once per delivered sample**, and the two are paired by timestamp at read time. Duplicating the commanded value onto every telemetry sample would multiply UHDR write volume and eMMC wear for no information. **The semantics are sample-and-hold: a command stands until a superseding event.** That is what makes the event form correct rather than merely cheaper — but it carries a hazard the sample form does not, and it is the same shape as the Rev 35 redaction finding (CLAUDE.md §5.1 rule 2): **in a hold-semantics log, the absence of a record is indistinguishable from the persistence of state.** A reader reconstructing commanded dose from a truncated log will carry the last command forward across a period when nothing was being commanded — producing a *false* divergence against delivered (or masking a real one), which is precisely the signal the cross-check exists to produce. **So the superseding events have to be enumerated and logged as deliberately as the commands**: new command, interval transition, pause, session end, abort, and safety-MCU cutoff. **The gap today is the ungraceful one.** `NP_ABORT_POWER = 5` is *"USB-C PD negotiation failed"* — an orderly, detected condition — and **no unclean-shutdown or dirty-session detection exists anywhere in project firmware** (the only brownout references in the tree are vendored NXP SDK register definitions). A power loss therefore leaves the last command record as the final entry with no terminator and nothing on the next boot marking the session truncated. Cheapest sufficient fix: a **dirty-session marker** set at session start and cleared at orderly end, so a set flag on boot means *"the previous session ended at an unknown time — do not hold its last command forward"*; the 22F hub supercapacitor (CLAUDE.md §4.5) may additionally buy enough write time to record the actual cut. A periodic keepalive would also bound the hold but trades back the write volume the event form was chosen to save. **This needs a new record type, not a new field**: there is no command-event record today, and `np_log_session_start()` writes only `session_uuid`, `start_unix` and `mods_active_mask` — **no protocol parameters at all**, though CLAUDE.md §5.1 lists *"protocol parameters used"* among UHDR contents and §4.6 Mode 4 promises *"EDF+ + parameter logs to app"*. So commanded dose is currently not reconstructable from the device log by itself, only via the app's copy of the authored protocol keyed on `session_uuid` — which is a gap worth closing on its own merits, independently of the cross-check; (3) the hub **cross-checks delivered against commanded and raises an SHDR divergence flag** — device condition, no biology, no new SHDR exposure. **This is what makes FMEA-M03-02 and FMEA-M03-03 real again.** Their genuine hazard class is *delivered charge diverging from what the monitor believes* — a driver stuck on, an electrode lifting. Written as ADC failures they are unmitigable fiction on this architecture; written as a commanded-versus-delivered divergence they are **detectable**, and the flag is the mitigation that lets M03-03 be re-scored honestly rather than carried on an unbuilt control. **The strong form stays on the table as a hardware ask** — Class C measuring delivered current independently — and is what to build if a regulator requires the interlock itself to be delivered-dose rather than commanded-dose.

  **What remains true regardless:** a monitor fed commanded current verifies what was *asked for*, never what reached the scalp. Every document that calls this a charge-*density* monitor over-claims it, and DI-SAFE-01, NP-SW-001 SW01-M03 and §3.3 all need to say which of the two it is.

  **What needs deciding, in order:** (1) adopt or reject the recommended resolution above — if adopted, the charge monitor is named a **commanded-dose** control, DI-SAFE-01 is re-scoped to say so, §3.3's failure modes are re-written around SPI/heartbeat failures and commanded-versus-delivered divergence rather than ADC failures, and the hub gains the cross-check and flag; **the delivered-dose alternative is a hardware decision (a Class C current-sense channel), not a privacy one**; (2) whether the 95% ramp-down margin is a requirement — it is implemented in Class B HD-tDCS (`np_hd_stim.c` §, `>= NP_HD_MAX_CHARGE_DENSITY_UC_CM2 * 0.95f`) but absent from the Class C monitor that both requirement documents place it in, and with tDCS's 30 s enforced ramp a 100% trip leaves no budget for ramp-down; (3) re-score FMEA-M03-01…03 against the module that exists. **Do not close this by editing prose alone** — FMEA-M03-03 is UNACCEPTABLE-initial, so its residual score depends on a real control being named.

  **Related, not duplicated:** `OI-CHARGE-05` owns the ceiling's value and the waveform question; `OI-CHARGE-01`'s unwired hub half owns the inertness. This item owns the gap between the hazard analysis and the design.

- [ ] **`OI-NPPS-LIMITS-01`: a shipped clinical protocol authors a VNS frequency this hardware cannot deliver (raised 2026-09-15, OPEN — decision owner: Clinical)** — `clinical-21-vns-stroke-rehab.npps`, *"taVNS — Stroke Motor Rehab (paired)"*, authors `frequency: 30Hz` on `vns_hrv`. **CLAUDE.md §3 locks auricular VNS at 1–25 Hz**, so all three app validators reject it and the safety MCU would never grant the channel. **Found by a test that did not exist until now** — `npps-predefined.test.ts` proved for a long time that every shipped protocol *parses*, and never that any of them would survive *hardware validation*. Adding that assertion while closing `OI-CHARGE-05` surfaced this immediately; it is unrelated to charge and predates that work. **Where the 30 Hz came from, and why it is not simply a typo:** the protocol cites **Dawson 2021 VNS-REHAB**, which used 30 Hz — but VNS-REHAB is the **implanted Vivistim** device on the cervical vagus, not transcutaneous auricular stimulation, and the protocol's own description already calls itself a *"non-invasive analogue"*. A source trial's parameter was transcribed across a change of device. **Deliberately NOT resolved by retuning it to 25 Hz.** Putting a number into a cited clinical protocol that the citation does not support is the same class of move as inflating a declared pad area to satisfy a ceiling — it makes the check pass and makes the document lie. What is owed is a clinical decision: (a) re-cite to taVNS evidence that actually used ≤25 Hz and adopt that frequency, (b) keep 30 Hz and withdraw the protocol from the shipped library until the hardware ceiling is revisited, or (c) establish that 25 Hz is therapeutically equivalent here and record *that* as the reason for the change. **Until then it is waived by name** in `npps-predefined.test.ts` — the waiver lists the exact failure string, so fixing the protocol or breaking a second one makes the test fail rather than silently absorbing either.

- [ ] **`OI-CHARGE-06`: the 150 mC/cm² DC ceiling is derived but not regulator-reviewed (raised 2026-09-15, OPEN — decision owner: Regulatory)** — `OI-CHARGE-05` (d) replaced a figure whose only source was this document set with one derived from two anchors outside it: the conventional large-pad human tDCS envelope in `docs/tdcs_database_full.csv` (204 of 237 studies state current, area and duration together; median 68.6 mC/cm², and the most-exposed large RCT protocol in the set is Brunoni ELECT-TDCS 2013/2017 at 144 mC/cm²), and a 35× margin to Liebetanz et al. 2009's measured rat epicranial DC lesion threshold of 5,240 mC/cm². **That is a real derivation where there was none, and it is still not a regulatory review.** What is owed: (a) Regulatory sign-off on 150 mC/cm² as the T1 wellness ceiling, and whether T2 should differ — the small-electrode tail of the literature (4 cm² montages at 300–600 mC/cm²) is excluded by this number, which is correct for T1 tDCS but is a decision about HD-tDCS the derivation does not make; (b) whether a **current-density** criterion (mA/cm²) should sit alongside it, as the Nitsche/Bikson safety framework states the limits — charge density alone is self-limiting on area but says nothing about instantaneous density, and the two criteria together are what bound the published envelope; (c) the relationship to `IEC 60601-2-10`, whose verification **VE-11 remains Open** (accredited-lab testing, Month 10–14). **Until this closes, `NP-DT-001` §3.2.1 marks the figure PROVISIONAL.** No code change is implied either way: the constant is one `#define` on the Class C MCU and never crosses SPI, and both app-side pre-flights read their own named constant. **DI-SAFE-01a's 40 µC/cm² per-phase figure is NOT in scope** — it is unchanged in value and now carries the Shannon/McCreery citation it always should have had.

- [ ] **`OI-CHARGE-07`: three electrode areas are firmware constants that have never been measured (raised 2026-09-15, OPEN — decision owner: Mechanical + Clinical)** — `OI-CHARGE-05` (b)'s per-phase ceiling divides by an electrode area, and three channels author none: BES/tACS pads, the VNS auricular clip pad, and the cervical VNS collar pad. `np_hub_config.h` now carries `NP_BES_ELECTRODE_AREA_MCM2` (25 cm²), `NP_VNS_ELECTRODE_AREA_MCM2` (0.5 cm²) and `NP_CVNS_ELECTRODE_AREA_MCM2` (2 cm²), each marked **PROVISIONAL — NOT MEASURED** in the same style as `NP_IMP_SENSE_R_OHM` and the NTC pin map. **Why these are legitimately firmware constants at all, when OI-CHARGE-04 exists to stop software assuming an electrode area:** the tDCS pad is a **consumable the user chooses**, so its area is a protocol-authoring fact and must travel in the signed descriptor; the BES pads, auricular clip and cervical collar are **fixed parts of the product** the user cannot substitute, so their area is a device property firmware may hold. That distinction is the line, not convenience. **The fail-safe direction is asserted rather than trusted**: `np_safety_spi_proto_tests.c` pins each to be non-zero (zero would fall back to the permissive 25 cm² default) and no larger than that default, so they may be revised **down** freely and **up** only against a measurement. **Margin today is large except in one place** — VNS is at 3% of the per-phase ceiling and cervical VNS at 3%, but 1 mA tACS at the 0.5 Hz band bottom is at **64%**, so the BES pad area is the one whose measurement actually matters. **Update 2026-09-23 (`NP-FW-HUB-001` Rev 3):** until this date these constants reached the safety MCU **only in sessions that also held tDCS or HD-tDCS** — in every other session the MCU enforced its 25 cm² fallback, so the per-phase check this item describes as dividing by 0.5 cm² did not, for a VNS-only session. Fixed (`REQ-FWHUB-37`, `np_chan_decl_tests`); the BES pad area is also now gated (`OI-MMSOCK-02`). The measurement is still owed, and the BES limb now belongs to the pad hardware `OI-MMSOCK-11` asks for. What is owed: measured areas from the mechanical drawings once the electrode parts are specified, and the same three constants mirrored in the app-side limits files (`hardwareLimits.ts`, `NPHardwareLimits.swift`, `NPHardwareLimits.kt`) which carry the identical provisional values.

- [ ] **`OI-FMEA-08`: both requirement documents specify a 95% ramp-down margin on the charge cutoff and neither implementation has one (raised 2026-09-15, OPEN — decision owner: Safety SW + Quality)** — `NP-SW-001` SW01-M03 and `NP-FMEA-001` §3.3 both stated an abort at *"95% of"* the ceiling, the margin *"provid[ing] margin for the abort ramp-down period"*. `np_charge_monitor.c` trips at **100%** (`>=` the derived limit) and clears the enable bit, which is abrupt by construction; the 95% figure exists only in Class B HD-tDCS (`np_hd_stim.c`). **This is not editorial.** tDCS enforces a 30 s minimum ramp, so a 100% trip leaves no budget to ramp within — the cutoff is a hard stop in the middle of a protocol that is specified to ramp. Two honest answers: implement a margin in Class C and specify what happens inside it (ramp to zero, then latch), or record that a charge cutoff is abrupt by design and that the ramp requirement does not apply to the safety path. Both documents now state that the margin is **absent** rather than implying it exists, so nothing is currently claimed that is not built. Raised while closing `OI-FMEA-07`, which found the discrepancy but could not resolve it without deciding the requirement.

- [ ] **`OI-FMEA-09`: FMEA-M03-02's residual score depends on a control that is specified but not built (raised 2026-09-15, OPEN — decision owner: Quality / Firmware)** — `NP-FMEA-001` Rev 7 re-authored the two retired ADC failure modes into the hazard they were standing in for: **delivered current diverging from commanded** (driver stuck on, electrode lifting, current source out of calibration). A commanded-dose monitor cannot detect it by construction, so the control is the hub's commanded-versus-delivered cross-check raising an SHDR **divergence flag** — device condition, no biology, reusing the `np_safety_imp_report_t` pattern. **That control does not exist yet**, so SW01-M03's residual rating in §4 is recorded as explicitly **conditional** rather than achieved. **Recording the dependency is the whole point**: Rev 5 found this section carrying an UNACCEPTABLE-initial hazard as mitigated by a control that had never been built, and an unstated dependency is how that happens. Scope, per `OI-FMEA-07`'s recommended resolution: the hub already writes delivered current and charge to UHDR via `NP_LOG_TAG_UHDR_STIM`; what is missing is (a) a **commanded-dose event record** — there is none today, and `np_log_session_start()` writes no protocol parameters at all, so commanded dose is not reconstructable from the device log; (b) the cross-check and flag; (c) the **dirty-session marker** the hold-semantics event log needs, because in a truncated log the absence of a record is indistinguishable from the persistence of state (CLAUDE.md §5.1 rule 2), and no unclean-shutdown detection exists anywhere in project firmware. **The strong form — a Class C current-sense ADC so the MCU measures delivered current itself — remains a hardware ask**, not a firmware one: ADC1 is fully allocated to six NTC and six impedance channels, and it lands on the pin budget `OI-HEXTILE-13` already contests.

- [x] **`OI-CONSENT-01`: Android has no consent dashboard, so five consent methods are unreachable from its UI — RESOLVED 2026-09-08** — iOS ships `ConsentDashboardView.swift`, from which `grantClinicianAccess`, `revokeClinicianAccess`, `acceptInvitation`, `declineInvitation` and `withdrawFromStudy` are all reachable. Android has no equivalent screen: its only consent UI is `ConsentOnboardingScreen.kt` plus a research-preferences section in `Screens.kt`, so those five methods are defined, unit-tested, and callable by nothing. **This is the CLAUDE.md Rev 37 shape at scale.** Rev 37 records exactly this defect for one method in the other direction — `withdrawBlanketResearchConsent()` was "correct and tested on both platforms" while nothing on iOS called it — and the consequence there was a silently skipped analytics teardown. The consequence here is narrower but real: a clinician grant made on iOS cannot be revoked from an Android device, and a study withdrawal (§6.2.5's guarantee that withdrawal granularity is preserved) has no Android path at all. **Not a data-loss or safety defect** — `ConsentStore.kt` is correct and the §6.0 teardown invariant holds on both platforms (verified: `updateResearchConsent` guards the blanket true→false transition on both, and `withdrawBlanketResearchConsent` calls `revokeResearchAnalytics` on both). It is a reachability gap. **Resolution:** build the Android consent dashboard to iOS parity, then delete the five per-platform waivers in `scripts/check-consent-reachability.ts` — the gate fails if a waiver is left in place once a caller exists, so the waivers cannot outlive the gap. Until then the gate prints all five on every run rather than tolerating them silently. **Closed 2026-09-08 by building the dashboard, not by widening the waiver.** `app/android/app/src/main/kotlin/life/neurone/app/ui/ConsentDashboardScreen.kt` is a port of iOS `ConsentDashboardView`: clinician grants (list · add · revoke, each revoke behind a confirmation naming the clinician and organisation), research-consent status with the L3 posture control and the §6.2 irreversibility notice, the study-participation audit trail with per-study withdrawal, and pending study invitations with an accept/decline detail screen whose accept path repeats the invitation's irreversibility notice (iOS ISC-79 parity). All five methods now have a non-test caller on Android and **all five waivers are deleted** — the gate reports no waiver in force. **Two deliberate differences from iOS, recorded rather than absorbed:** (i) L3 posture stays a switch on Android rather than iOS's one-way "Stop pre-approving studies" button, because Android could already re-grant blanket consent from this tab and removing that would be a regression — turning it *off* now goes through the same confirmation copy iOS shows and the same named `withdrawBlanketResearchConsent()` path, so the §6.0 teardown is explicit at the call site as well as guarded at the store; (ii) the grant form shows the tier's derived can-see / cannot-see UHDR element lists (§6.1 minimum necessary) where iOS shows a use-case picker, because **iOS discards that picker's selection when it builds the grant** — `approvedElements` comes from the tier on both platforms, so porting the picker would have ported a control that decides nothing. That iOS defect is not fixed here and is raised as `OI-CONSENT-04`. **The L2 category list on the dashboard is read-only** (status checkboxes, not editing controls): L2 edits belong to Research Preferences and must commit through `updateResearchConsent`, which is what carries the §6.2.5 transition guard — a second write path would bypass it. **One blocker found and fixed to get here:** the ten consent-surface format keys still carried iOS-only `%@` placeholders in canonical (`DASHBOARD_ACCESS_FORMAT`, `DASHBOARD_REVOKE_MESSAGE_FORMAT`, `INVITATION_STUDY_ID_FORMAT`, …), which `sync-locales` copies through verbatim because `%@` is not a `{0}` placeholder — Android's `String.format` throws `UnknownFormatConversionException` on it, so those keys were unusable from Kotlin. They are now canonical `{0}`/`{1}` in all 11 locales, generating `%1$s` for Android and `%1$@` for Apple; iOS call sites are unchanged because positional and non-positional `%@` consume the same arguments in the same order. **Ten further `%@` keys remain** on session, setup, consumable and zone screens Android has not reached yet — tracked as `OI-I18N-01`. **Not verified by compiler:** this environment has no Android SDK, so the screen is unbuilt here; `:app:testDebugUnitTest` in `.github/workflows/android-ci.yml` is what compiles it, and every `R.string` reference was checked against the generated `strings.xml` by hand first. Full record in `completed-decisions.md`.

- [x] **`OI-CONSENT-02`: §6.1's clinician access-expansion workflow is unimplemented on both platforms — RESOLVED 2026-09-09** — `expandClinicianAccess()` existed in both `ConsentStore.swift` and `ConsentStore.kt` with **no caller anywhere, not even a unit test**. §6.1 specifies the workflow it belongs to in detail — differential consent document → persistent user notification → user approves/denies/asks questions, with retroactive and prospective access presented as separate decisions — and none of that surface existed. **Resolution (1) was taken — build the workflow — not (2), remove the method.** Two things decided it. §6.1 is locked and names the workflow in detail, so deleting the method would have left a locked specification with nothing behind it and no record of the gap outside this file. And `OI-CONSENT-01` closed on 2026-09-08, so both platforms now have a consent dashboard to host it; the reason to defer was gone the day before this was picked up. **The method was worse than unimplemented, which is what made "remove or build" not a wash.** `ClinicianConsentGrant.approvedElements` derives from `tier`, and a tier is timeless: setting it — the entire body of the old method — hands the clinician every newly added element over *every session ever recorded*. It did not merely lack §6.1's retroactive decision; it took that decision silently, and took it yes. No model on either platform could have expressed the other answer, so building the workflow had to start at the data model, not the UI. **What landed.** `ClinicianAccessScope` (elements · effective-from · `includesPriorData`) makes a grant a sequence of access decisions rather than a single timeless tier, and `elementsVisible(forDataRecordedAt:)` / `elementsVisibleForDataRecordedOn()` is the predicate that makes the retroactive answer mean something. A grant with no scopes — every grant written before this — falls back to *the tier's elements, from the grant date, prior data included*, which is exactly what a bare tier has always meant, so no existing grant is silently re-scoped. `ConsentEngine.accessDifferential()` is the differential consent document (new · already visible · still withheld); it returns nothing for a change that adds nothing, for one that would *remove* an element — a re-scope, not a widening — and for either side being the Research tier, whose element set is IRB-defined per study descriptor and whose emptiness must never be read as a set. `ClinicianAccessExpansionRequest` is the persistent notification, persisted beside the grants and surviving a question, because asking is not deciding. The store gained `addExpansionRequest` · `approveExpansion(requestID:includePriorData:)` · `denyExpansion` · `askQuestionAboutExpansion`, and **`expandClinicianAccess` is now private on both platforms** — which is why it no longer appears in `check-consent-reachability.ts`'s SURFACE. That is a stronger guarantee than the declaration it replaces: the gate can say a public method has no caller today, never that nothing may call it tomorrow. Both UIs ask the two decisions **on separate steps** — approving answers only the forward-looking question, and the history question is asked afterwards with both answers as buttons of equal weight — because a single screen with an Approve button and a history checkbox presents one decision with a modifier on it, not two decisions. A "from today onwards only" answer stays visible afterwards on the grant row (`DASHBOARD_FORWARD_ONLY_FORMAT`), or the user could not tell which of the two answers they gave. 12 new unit tests per platform. **Two successors raised, both about halves this deliberately did not build:** `OI-CONSENT-05` (nothing ingests a request or delivers a question — the clinician-portal channel does not exist) and `OI-CONSENT-06` (the *initial* grant's retroactive posture is still not separately elicited). Full record in `completed-decisions.md`.

- [x] **`OI-CONSENT-03`: study invitations have no ingestion path — RESOLVED 2026-09-10** — `addInvitation()` was called only from unit tests on both platforms. §6.3's per-project contact workflow has NeurOne generating an eligible-patient list and sending a personalised invitation, which the device must then receive; there was no sync layer, no study-descriptor fetch, and no caller. **Resolved by building the device's half of the ingestion, not by giving the old method a caller** — because, in the `OI-CONSENT-02` shape, the method was worse than unimplemented. `addInvitation(StudyInvitation)` took a *finished* invitation — approved element list, plain-language "what they CANNOT see" prose and the irreversibility notice — on trust from whatever called it, and thereby took four decisions silently and took each in the affirmative: that §5.3's signature on the study descriptor need not be checked, that §5.3's k≥10 and ≥1-week anonymisation floors need not be met, that the user's own L1/L2/L3 state need not be consulted, and that the party asking for access may write the sentence describing what it cannot see. It also held invitations **in memory only**, so an accepted study's participation record outlived the invitation recording the acceptance and the same descriptor could be ingested and accepted again after a restart. **What landed.** `StudyDescriptor` (§5.3 step 1 — studyID, title, categories, requested elements, k, date-rounding, signature) is what enters the device; the `StudyInvitation` the user reads is *derived* from it after `StudyDescriptorVerifier` verifies the signature, and `cannotLearn` is the complement of the approved set computed on-device. `ConsentEngine.admit()` is the gate: signature first, then §5.3's floors, then L1 (§6.2.1 makes a contact method the precondition for *every* delivery path), then a per-study already-decided check (§5.3 / §6.3 step 6 — withdrawal blocks future descriptor processing), then posture. **The posture is the finding that made this a data-model change.** §6.2 locks that an L3 user "still receives per-study *engagement* notifications, **not consent requests**", so the same study reaches an L2 and an L3 user as two different things with **opposite defaults on silence** — an unanswered consent request means *not participating*, an unread engagement notification means *participating* — and one shape for both had to pick one default for everyone. `StudyInvitation.Posture` holds both; an engagement notification records participation at ingestion and its "decline" is a withdrawal. §6.3 step 4's third response, *Ask a question*, now exists (`askQuestionAboutInvitation`, leaving the invitation open, the `askQuestionAboutExpansion` rule); invitations persist; the participation record carries the real descriptor hash instead of the literal string `"pending"`. 17 new unit tests per platform. **What deliberately did not land, and is now `OI-CONSENT-07`:** the transport. There is still no descriptor fetch and no signing key, so `ingestStudyDescriptor` has no non-test caller — but it is **no longer open by default**: with no verifier injected it refuses every descriptor as `verifierUnavailable`, so the transport cannot be wired up without also supplying §5.3's signature check. Full record in `completed-decisions.md`.

- [ ] **`OI-CONSENT-07`: the NeurOne study-service transport does not exist, so a descriptor can be admitted but never fetched, and a question never delivered (raised 2026-09-10, OPEN — decision owner: App Lead)** — successor to `OI-CONSENT-03`, and the sibling of `OI-CONSENT-05`. `OI-CONSENT-03` built everything §5.3 and §6.3 put on the device: the descriptor model, the signature-verification port, the anonymisation-floor and consent gating, the two postures, the three responses, persistence. Three things it connects to are missing, all of them server-side contracts rather than app work. **Inbound:** nothing fetches study descriptors — `ingestStudyDescriptor()` has no non-test caller on either platform. **The signing key:** `StudyDescriptorVerifier`'s only implementation is `RefusingStudyDescriptorVerifier`, which answers `unavailable` for every descriptor, so on every device today ingestion refuses everything. That is deliberate and is the useful half of this item: it makes "wire up a transport and skip the signature check" unrepresentable rather than merely discouraged, so closing this needs the key distribution decided, not assumed. **Outbound:** §6.3 step 4's *Ask a question* ("secure message to a NeurOne liaison, 2 business day response") has nowhere to send the question — `askQuestionAboutInvitation()` records that one was asked and leaves the invitation open, and both UIs tell the user to contact NeurOne rather than implying a message was delivered, which is honest and is not the workflow §6.3 describes. **Not urgent and not a defect:** the research portal these serve is not built either, and every decision path is exercisable from the dashboard. Closing it needs the study service's device-facing API and its signing-key distribution specified first — at which point this and `OI-CONSENT-05` likely share one transport, though not one counterparty (NeurOne inviting a subject to a study, versus a clinician widening an existing grant). **Partly addressed 2026-09-14, and deliberately not closed (issue #338).** The device-facing API is now specified — `NP-SW-PORTAL-API-001`, which also concludes that this item and `OI-CONSENT-05` **share one transport**, on three properties each would otherwise have to re-derive: pull-only delivery, admission by signature rather than by transport, and a response identical whether or not the user has consented to anything. **The verification side is built; the key is not, and the key is the item.** §4(a): the verifying key must ship *with the app build*, pinned, and may not be fetched over the channel it authenticates — a channel delivering both the object and its key proves only that one party sent both, which TLS already proves. So this is a key-management item, not a coding one, and writing an HTTP client would not have touched it. **What landed (§4a):** `StudyDescriptorCanonicalForm` on both platforms — the one schema decision the spec takes, because *"descriptors are cryptographically signed"* is not implementable until something says what is signed — and `Ed25519StudyDescriptorVerifier` over it. The form is **length-prefixed rather than delimited** (`studyTitle` is server-supplied text and can contain the separator; a delimiter-joined encoding lets a crafted title shift field boundaries so that one signature covers two descriptors — both suites carry that forgery attempt), **sorts sets and preserves list order**, and **names categories and elements by a stable wire spelling** rather than by whichever serializer each platform happens to use: iOS `rawValue`, and on Android an explicit `wireName` with `displayName` now derived from it, so localization reaching `:core` later cannot move the wire. **One finding came out of building it:** iOS modelled the issue date as an instant and Android as a day, so the canonical form must use the coarser — **time of day is not covered by the signature**, and an issuer must not rely on it to distinguish two descriptors. The day renders in UTC on a fixed Gregorian calendar so a descriptor cannot verify in one time zone and fail in another. Ed25519, and the **raw 32 bytes** as the key on both platforms even though the JDK wants SPKI, so "the NeurOne study-signing key" is one artifact rather than two. `RefusingStudyDescriptorVerifier` remains the `ConsentStore` default, and an absent, malformed or wrong-length key yields **no verifier at all** rather than one that throws at the first descriptor — a misconfigured build falls back to refusing. **Still open, and what closing needs:** who holds the study-review signing key, how it is rotated, and how it reaches an app build. No endpoint or schema beyond the signing form is specified, on purpose (§7). Record in `completed-decisions.md`, 2026-09-14.

- [x] **`OI-CONSENT-04`: the iOS clinician-grant form asks for use cases and then throws the answer away (raised 2026-09-08 — RESOLVED 2026-09-13)** — `NewClinicianGrantView` in `app/ios/NeurOne/Views/ConsentDashboardView.swift` renders `ConsentEngine.useCaseLibrary` as a toggle list and holds the selection in `selectedUseCases`, but the `ClinicianConsentGrant` it builds carries only the tier: `selectedUseCases` is read by nothing, and `approvedElements` derives from `tier.uhdrElements`. So the user is asked a question §6.1 makes load-bearing — *clinicians select use cases, never data elements* — and the answer changes no access decision. `ConsentEngine.minimumNecessaryElements(for:)` and `consentDocument(...)`, the two functions that would consume it, have no caller either. **Found while building the Android dashboard for `OI-CONSENT-01`**, which is why that screen shows the tier's derived can-see / cannot-see element lists instead of porting the picker. Not a data-access defect — the tier ceiling is what actually gates, and it is the conservative reading of any use-case set the picker could produce — but it is a consent-surface honesty defect, and it is the shape §6.1 warns about. **Decide between:** (1) make the grant carry its use-case set and derive `approvedElements` from `minimumNecessaryElements`, which makes the picker load-bearing, narrows most grants below their tier ceiling, and needs a `ClinicianConsentGrant` schema change plus an Android port; or (2) remove the picker from the iOS form and present the tier's derived elements, matching what Android now does. Either way the two orphaned `ConsentEngine` functions resolve with it. **Resolution (1) was taken — make the picker load-bearing — on both platforms.** The grant now carries `useCaseIDs`/`useCaseIds` (what the user consented to, in §6.1's own terms) **and** an explicit initial `ClinicianAccessScope` holding the elements `ConsentEngine.initialAccessScope()` derived from them. Both are kept because they answer different questions and one of them must not be recomputed: **the elements are frozen at grant time**, so adding an element to a library entry cannot reach backwards and widen every grant already made, with nobody asked. The derived set is **clamped to the tier**, which stays the ceiling and the thing the subscription is priced at; `ConsentEngine.useCases(availableFor:)` only offers use cases the tier can carry, and the clamp is belt-and-braces for a caller that skips it. **The library is ported to Android and now carries keys, not English** (`CLINICIAN_USECASE_<ID>_NAME`/`_DESC`, the `MODALITY_<ID>` precedent): `:core` is pure-JVM and cannot reference `R.string` at all, so English in the shared table would be English no platform could translate — CLAUDE.md §17's module-level-tables-hold-keys rule, which is also what retired the `TODO(localization)` at the top of `ConsentEngine.swift`. The two orphaned functions resolved as the item said they would: `minimumNecessaryElements(for:)` is what the form derives from, and `consentDocument(...)` is rendered live under the picker — minus `plainLanguageSummary`, an English paragraph assembled from element `rawValue`s, which are the persisted Codable values rather than display text. The document now carries **no prose of its own**, the rule `ClinicianAccessDifferential` was already built under. Android's grant form gains the picker it deliberately did not port; the can-see / cannot-see lists it showed from the tier now follow the selection. Record in `completed-decisions.md`, 2026-09-13.

- [x] **`OI-CONSENT-05`: the clinician-portal channel does not exist, so an access-expansion request can be answered but never received or replied to (raised 2026-09-09 — RESOLVED 2026-09-13 as far as the device side can be resolved; two named successors stay open)** — `OI-CONSENT-02` built §6.1's user-facing half: the differential consent document, the persistent notification, and the two separately-asked decisions. The two ends it connects to are missing. **Inbound:** `addExpansionRequest()` is called only from unit tests on both platforms — the same shape as `OI-CONSENT-03`, and the same absent sync layer in spirit, but a different channel and a different counterparty (a clinician widening an existing grant, not NeurOne inviting a subject to a study), so it does not fold into that item. **Outbound:** §6.1's third response, *asks questions*, has nowhere to send the question. `askQuestionAboutExpansion()` records that one was asked and leaves the request pending, and both UIs say plainly that the user should contact their clinician rather than implying a message was delivered — honest, and not the workflow §6.1 describes. **Not urgent and not a defect:** the clinician-facing portal these serve is not built either, and the decision paths are exercisable from the dashboard today. Recorded so the empty halves are visible rather than inferred from a passing test suite. Closing it needs the clinician portal's device-facing API specified first — at which point `OI-CONSENT-03` and this item likely share one transport. **Closed the way the item said it had to be: by specifying the portal's device-facing API first.** `NP-SW-PORTAL-API-001` does that, and confirms this item's own guess that `OI-CONSENT-03` and this one **share one transport** — not for convenience but because each would otherwise have to re-derive three properties: pull-only delivery, admission by signature rather than by transport, and a response that is identical whether or not the user has consented to anything. **What landed in code is a port, not a transport.** `ClinicianPortalChannel` with `RefusingClinicianPortalChannel` as the only shipped implementation, and `ClinicianPortalSync` ingesting into `ConsentStore` in an order that is load-bearing: **identity, then the device's own records, then the differential**, so a forged request naming a grant this device never issued is refused *as forged*, never as unknown — a refusal whose shape varies with which grants the device holds is a way of telling the caller which grants it holds (§5.1 rule 2). `addExpansionRequest` moves from `pending` to a new `ingest` declaration in `check-consent-reachability.ts`, which names the ingesting file and fails if it is missing or does not call the method; declaring it `ui` would have passed that gate on the strength of a caller that is not a UI. **Two things are deliberately still missing and are named rather than faked:** a clinician-identity anchor (§4c — until one exists there is nothing to check a request against, and a verifier that said *verified* on that basis would be asserting what nobody can know), and an answer for §6.1's *asks questions*, whose text is user-authored content about the user's own care and therefore UHDR — E2E to the clinician or it does not ship, never relayed as plaintext through a NeurOne service (§6). **No endpoint or schema is specified on purpose**: writing a URL and a JSON body before the key question is settled would make the unsolved part look solved. Record in `completed-decisions.md`, 2026-09-13.

- [x] **`OI-CONSENT-06`: an *initial* clinician grant's retroactive posture is never separately elicited (raised 2026-09-09 — RESOLVED 2026-09-13)** — §6.1's *"retroactive and prospective access presented as separate consent decisions"* is now honoured for **expansion** (`OI-CONSENT-02`) and not for the first grant. Granting a clinician Monitor access through `NewClinicianGrantView` / `NewClinicianGrantScreen` still produces a grant with no scopes, which `effectiveScopes` reads as *the tier's elements, from the grant date, prior data included* — one answer to a question the user was never asked. **That fallback was deliberate and should not be read as the decision:** it is precisely what a bare `tier` has always meant, so introducing the scope model did not silently re-scope any existing grant, and changing the initial default is a change of behaviour that belongs in its own decision rather than smuggled in beside a workflow build. **Two candidate resolutions.** (1) **Ask the same second question in the grant form** — the two-step control already exists and would be reused verbatim; costs a step on the most common consent flow in the app. (2) **Default an initial grant to prospective-only**, on *when in doubt → UHDR* (CLAUDE.md §5.1), and let a user widen it afterwards through the expansion workflow, which is exactly what that workflow is for. (2) is the cheaper and the more conservative, and it is a real change to what a grant means, so it is not a code tidy. **Not a defect today**, because no consumer of `elementsVisible(forDataRecordedAt:)` exists yet — no clinician-facing read path is built, on any platform — which is also the reason to settle it before one is. **Resolution (1) was taken — ask the same second question in the grant form — not (2), default to prospective-only.** (2) was the cheaper and the more conservative of the two, and that is what decided against it: *when in doubt → UHDR* is the right default for a field nobody will ever be asked about, and the wrong answer to a question the specification says to ask. §6.1 does not say retroactive access should be narrow; it says the two decisions are **always presented as separate decisions**, and a default — either default — presents neither. The two-step control from the expansion flow is reused verbatim on both platforms: the history question comes afterwards and on its own, with both answers as buttons of equal weight and neither preselected, because a checkbox beside a Grant button presents one decision with a modifier on it. It costs a step on the most common consent flow in the app, which is the cost the item named and the one worth paying. **The `effectiveScopes` fallback stays and is now reached only by grants written before this** — *the tier's elements, from the grant date, prior data included* is what a bare tier has always meant, and a grant made under it has to keep meaning that. Record in `completed-decisions.md`, 2026-09-13.

- [x] **`OI-I18N-01`: ten canonical keys still carry iOS-only `%@` placeholders, and any Android use of one crashes — RESOLVED 2026-09-13** — CLAUDE.md §17 makes `{0}`/`{1}` the canonical placeholder form, which `sync-locales.ts` renders as `%1$@` for Apple and `%1$s` for Android. Ten keys predate that rule and still hold a literal `%@`: `CONSUMABLE_ORDER_PACK_FORMAT`, `SESSION_COHERENCE_A11Y_FORMAT`, `SESSION_COHERENCE_SCORE_A11Y_FORMAT`, `SESSION_COHERENCE_VALUE_FORMAT`, `SESSION_RMSSD_VALUE_FORMAT`, `SETUP_ERROR_IMPEDANCE_FAILED`, `SETUP_ERROR_ZONE_MISSING`, `SETUP_IMPEDANCE_FAIL_A11Y`, `SETUP_IMPEDANCE_PASS_A11Y` and `ZONE_ANNOUNCE_NO_TYPE`. `%@` matches no `{n}` pattern, so both generators copy it through verbatim; it happens to be exactly what Apple's `String(format:)` wants, which is why nothing has failed. **On Android it is a crash, not a mis-render** — `Resources.getString(id, args)` runs `java.util.Formatter`, and `%@` is an unknown conversion (`UnknownFormatConversionException`). Every one of these keys is referenced only from iOS today, so nothing is broken now; the first Android screen to reach one throws. The ten consent-surface keys in the same state were converted on 2026-09-08 for `OI-CONSENT-01` — conversion is mechanical and iOS-neutral (positional and non-positional `%@` consume the same arguments in the same order), so this is not a hard item, but it touches the session, setup, consumable and zone screens rather than consent and was left rather than widened into that change. **Also decide whether `check-locale-strings.ts` should reject `%@` in canonical outright**, which is what would stop the next one being written. **Closed 2026-09-13 by converting the keys and closing the hole that let them exist.** All ten are canonical `{0}` in all eleven locale files, generating `%1$@` for Apple and `%1$s` for Android. **Two further keys were converted with them**, because scoping the fix to the literal `%@` spelling would have left the identical crash reachable through the positional one: `ZONE_ANNOUNCE_WITH_TYPE` (`%1$@, %2$@`) and `ZONE_SOCKET_POSITION_LABEL` (`Socket %1$d — %2$@`). A gate that rejects `%@` but accepts `%1$@` is not a gate. **Every conversion in a touched value had to convert, not just the `@` ones** — `sync-locales.ts` escapes a literal `%` only once a `{n}` is present, so leaving `%1$d` beside a new `{1}` would have emitted `%%1$d` and rendered the specifier itself; this is why `ZONE_SOCKET_POSITION_LABEL` became `Socket {0} — {1}` whole. **One iOS call site changed**, and it is the one the conversion actually moved: `ZoneModuleInfo.label(for:)` passed `Int(socketID)` to what was `%1$d` and is now `%1$@`, so the socket number is converted with `String(socketID)` at the call site per §17.2. The other eleven call sites already passed `String` (`NPNumberFormatter.decimal0/1`, `packDisplayPrice`, joined label lists, `channelNames`), so they are unchanged — positional and non-positional `%@` consume the same arguments in the same order, and `ZONE_ANNOUNCE_WITH_TYPE`'s generated Apple value is byte-identical to what it was. **The gate is the point of the item, and it is in.** `checkInterpolationSyntax` in `scripts/check-locale-strings.ts` now rejects any Apple-only `@` conversion — `%@` and `%n$@` — in a canonical value, with ten self-test cases (`--self-test`) driving the rule directly rather than through `scanSource`, so the rule is proven to reject as well as to pass. Verified by reintroducing `%@` into `ZONE_ANNOUNCE_NO_TYPE` and watching CI exit 1. **Android resolution verified at runtime, not only at build:** the generated `values/strings.xml` was driven through `java.util.Formatter` — the same code path `Resources.getString(id, args)` takes — and all twelve keys resolve with no leftover specifier, while the two pre-fix values throw `UnknownFormatConversionException` on that same path, which is the crash this item predicted. **Not verified by compiler:** this environment has no Swift toolchain, so the iOS call-site change is unbuilt here; `ios-ci.yml` is what compiles it. **Deliberately not widened:** fourteen keys still carry `%d` / `%.1f` / `%1$d`, which are valid conversions in both `String(format:)` and `java.util.Formatter` — they block a translator from reordering arguments and mis-render at worst, rather than crashing, and are raised separately as `OI-I18N-02`. Full record in `completed-decisions.md`.

- [x] **`OI-I18N-02`: fourteen canonical keys carry printf conversions rather than `{n}`, so a translator cannot reorder their arguments — RESOLVED 2026-09-23 (GitHub Issue #191)** — `OI-I18N-01` removed every Apple-only `@` conversion from canonical and gated it. It deliberately left the rest of the printf family, which is a different defect: `%d`, `%.1f` and `%1$d` are valid conversions in both `String(format:)` and `java.util.Formatter`, so they render on both platforms and crash on neither. What they cost is §17.2's actual purpose. A canonical `{0}` is **positional by construction** — the generator emits `%1$s` / `%1$@`, so a translation may put the arguments in whatever order its grammar needs. A bare `%d` is consumed in source order, and `SETUP_ZONE_SUMMARY` ("{0} of {1} sockets populated" as `%1$d of %2$d`) is exactly the shape that breaks: every locale file holds the English string today (translation is #191), and the first translator to move an argument gets a silently wrong sentence on one platform. They also render literally on web, whose `t()` substitutes `{n}` and nothing else — so these keys are already iOS-only in practice, the same latent single-platform state `OI-I18N-01` was. **The keys:** `ADAPT_OFFSET_HMS`, `ADAPT_OFFSET_MS`, `CONSUMABLE_SESSIONS_LEFT_FORMAT`, `CONSUMABLE_SESSIONS_USED_FORMAT`, `CONSUMABLE_SNOOZE_FORMAT`, `HISTORY_AVERAGE_COHERENCE_1F`, `HISTORY_COHERENCE_1F`, `SESSION_RMSSD_FORMAT`, `SESSION_ZONE_MODULE_COUNT`, `SESSION_ZONE_MODULE_FAULTS`, `SETUP_ELECTRODE_LABEL`, `SETUP_SOCKET_LABEL`, `SETUP_ZONE_SUMMARY`, `ZONE_SOCKET_LABEL`. Recover the list with `%(?:\d+\$)?[-#+0,(]*\d*(?:\.\d+)?[diouxXeEfgGcs]` over `locales/en.json` — a looser `%.` pattern also catches the literal percent in `{0}% exceeds limit` and the `%MT` unit, which are correct as they stand. **Not mechanical the way `OI-I18N-01` was**: `{0}` generates `%1$@` on Apple, which takes an object, so every numeric argument needs `String(…)` at the call site (§17.2) — the conversion is only safe alongside a call-site audit, which is why it was not folded into the `@` fix. Decide then whether `checkInterpolationSyntax` should reject every `%` conversion in canonical outright, which is what would make the rule complete rather than `@`-shaped. **Closed 2026-09-23, before translation started, so no key has to be translated twice (#191).** All fourteen are canonical `{0}`/`{1}` in all eleven locale files (every locale still held the untranslated English, so no translation was touched). **Every call site was audited and converts its own number:** iOS passes `String(…)` for each integer argument (`ConsumableView`, `SessionView`, `SetupView`, `HardwareSetupManager`, `ZoneModuleInfo` ×2), and `AdaptiveAdjustmentsCard.offsetLabel` does the zero padding that `%02d` used to do, because `{n}` renders as `%n$@` and pads nothing. The two `HISTORY_*_1F` keys were the only ones Android reads; `HistoryScreen.kt` now passes `"%.1f".format(it)` as the argument, the pattern `SessionScreen` already uses, which formats with the default locale as before. `SETUP_ZONE_SUMMARY` is now `{0} of {1} sockets populated`, so a translator can put the total first. **The rule is now complete, not `@`-shaped:** `checkInterpolationSyntax` rejects every printf conversion in a canonical value (the pattern above, with no space in the flag set, so `{0}% exceeds` and `%MT` still pass), and five new self-test cases cover it. Verified by putting `%d` back into `SETUP_ELECTRODE_LABEL` and watching the gate exit 1. **Not verified by compiler:** no Swift toolchain or Android SDK here, so `ios-ci.yml` and `android-ci.yml` compile the call sites. The generated `%1$s:%2$s:%3$s` (Android) and `%1$@:%2$@:%3$@` (Apple) values were checked by hand. Full record in `completed-decisions.md`.
- [x] **`OI-I18N-03`: the locale key set carries only `_ONE` / `_OTHER` plural members, so Russian and Arabic cannot express their plural forms — RESOLVED 2026-09-23 (GitHub #191)** — CLDR gives Russian `one / few / many / other` and Arabic `zero / one / two / few / many / other`. The locale files cannot hold them. `check-locale-strings.ts` requires every locale to carry exactly en.json's key set, so `ru.json` cannot add a `_FEW` member that English does not have. Web's `tPlural` also chooses only `_ZERO` / `_ONE` / `_OTHER` from the count, without `Intl.PluralRules`. Nothing crashes: Android `<plurals>` and Apple string-catalog variations fall back to `other` for a missing category. But a Russian translator given only the `_ONE` / `_OTHER` pair from `scripts/translations.ts export` must pick one form for 2, 5 and 21 alike, and that is wrong for at least one of them. English, the other Latin-script locales, Hindi and Bengali (`one / other`), and Indonesian and Chinese (`other` only) are unaffected. **What resolving it needs:** (i) the parity rule has to let a locale carry the extra CLDR categories of a plural family whose base is in en.json, without letting it carry any other extra key; (ii) `sync-locales.ts` has to emit those categories to Android `<plurals>` and to Apple variations, and it already accepts the suffixes; (iii) web's `tPlural` has to choose the category with `Intl.PluralRules(locale)`; (iv) the export has to issue the missing categories to the translator. **Resolve it before `ru` or `ar` is sent for translation**; the other eight locales are not blocked. **Closed 2026-09-23 with all four parts, in the same PR as the ledger.** (i) Parity: `localeParityErrors` in `check-locale-strings.ts` requires every en.json key and allows an extra key only when it is one of the locale's own CLDR categories (`Intl.PluralRules`) on a family en.json defines. (ii) `sync-locales.ts` needed no change: both generators already emit every category a locale file carries, on bases taken from en.json. (iii) Web `tPlural` now uses `pluralKey()`, which selects by `Intl.PluralRules(locale)` after an explicit `_ZERO`, and falls back to `_OTHER` for a category the file does not carry. (iv) The export issues the missing categories with example counts, translated from `_OTHER`, and skips members the language never selects (`_ONE` in Chinese and Indonesian). An extra category may exist only as a verified translation; the gate fails one with no ledger entry. Eighteen new gate self-test cases and six new web tests cover it. A plural member may leave out the count `{0}` only when its category matches exactly one count (Arabic dual "سطران"); Russian `one` may not. **One visible consequence:** until they are translated, Chinese and Indonesian render the English `_OTHER` for a count of 1 on web, as Android and Apple already did, because CLDR gives those languages no `one`. Full record in `completed-decisions.md`. **Correction (2026-09-24, wording only):** the problem statement above wrongly counts "the other Latin-script locales" as unaffected. CLDR gives French, Latin-American Spanish and Catalan `one / many / other`, where `many` selects exact multiples of a million ("1 000 000 de lignes"). The fix already covered them, because the gate and the export read categories from `Intl.PluralRules` and not from a list: `fr`, `es-419` and `ca` each get seven `_MANY` keys in their export. Only `localization.md` §17.2 and §17.7 and the parity rule's doc comment in `check-locale-strings.ts` were changed to say so; no behaviour changed.
- [x] **OI-TACS-01: clinical tACS is specified at 21 channels but the hub wire format addresses only 16 — RESOLVED 2026-09-07 (NP-NPPS-REF-001 Rev 15)** — The 2026-08-05 decision "T2 clinical tACS driver: 21 channels, one per cap electrode" (superseding "16-ch arbitrary waveform") is implemented for HD-tDCS — `NP_HD_DRIVER_CHANNELS = 21U` in `firmware/sloreta_hdtdcs/include/np_hd_config.h`, and `k_driver_channel[]` maps all 21 cap electrodes without aliasing — but **not for clinical tACS itself**. `np_mod_clin_tacs_params_t` (`firmware/hub_control/include/np_hub_types.h`) carries `channel_mask_lo` (channels 0–7) and `channel_mask_hi` (channels 8–15) and nothing else, so channels 16–20 cannot be enabled over the hub wire. Every encoder follows the struct: `encodeClinicalTacs()` in `app/web/src/lib/hubCompiler.ts` clamps to 16, the web `NumberField` caps at 16 and the iOS `Stepper` at `2...16`. Nothing validates `channel_count` in any runtime, so a `.npps` file may author 21 and be silently reduced to 16 at compile time. **Not a safety issue** — the clamp is downward, and the T2 module drivers are still stubs (`np_mod_t2_stubs.c`), so nothing decodes the struct today. It is a capability gap: the platform cannot deliver the channel count its own specification claims. **Closing it is a hub wire-format change, not an app-side edit:** `np_mod_clin_tacs_params_t` needs a third mask byte for channels 16–20 (7 → 8 bytes), with matching changes in the three app encoders, the `size: 7` assertion in `app/web/src/lib/hubCompiler.test.ts`, and the UI bounds above. Deliberately **not** attempted as part of the 2026-09-07 consistency pass, which corrected the stale "16-ch" statements in NP-DT-001 (DI-PERF-08), NP-HEX-ZM-001, NP-HEX-ZM-ISA, NP-PWR-BUDGET-001 and NP-NPPS-REF-001 §4.12 to 21 and recorded the encoding limit in-place at all three clamp sites.
